B routine priorityPublic-health news

Takeda's dengue vaccine wins India's first approval - Vietnam Investment Review - VIR

News Brief
India's Drug Controller General has granted marketing authorization for Takeda's dengue vaccine TAK-003, marking the country's first approval for a dengue vaccine. Announced on July 21, 2026, the authorization allows for the prevention of dengue in individuals aged four to 60, regardless of prior exposure. This decision addresses a significant public health challenge in India, where reported cases exceeded 233,000 in 2024 and the disease burden is estimated to be in the tens of millions annually. With transmission no longer confined to the monsoon season and cases rising over 11-fold in two decades, the vaccine offers a new tool for sustained prevention. Takeda will work with authorities to facilitate the vaccine's availability, supported by global clinical data and World Health Organization prequalification.
Original source text (English)
Advertisement Subscribe now Log in July 23, 2026 03:14 E-Paper E-Magazine Corporate CEO Talk Biz Link PR Newswire Media OutReach Newswire Investing M&A Outbound Investment Investing in Haiphong Your Consultant KPMG PwC Indochine Counsel Vilasia Green Growth Energy Efficiency Rethinking Plastics BASF Property Green Buildings Industrial Properties Money Insurance Smart Payment Trends Stock Monitor Infographics Timeout Travel Hotels & Restaurants The Insider Entertainment Lifestyle Sports Photos Society Swing for the Kids CSR World News Corporate CEO Talk Biz Link PR Newswire Media OutReach Newswire Takeda's dengue vaccine wins India's first approval July 22, 2026 | 10:04 (0) user say Share Email Print India's Drug Controller General granted marketing authorisation for Takeda's dengue vaccine TAK-003, becoming the country's first approved dengue vaccine for individuals aged four to sixty. NEW DELHI, July 21, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Takeda today announced that India's Drug Controller General of India (DCGI) has granted marketing authorization for Takeda's dengue vaccine (TAK-003) for the prevention of dengue disease in individuals 4 (four) to 60 (sixty) years of age. TAK-003 is India's first-ever approved dengue vaccine and was approved for use in individuals regardless of previous dengue exposure, without the need for pre-vaccination testing. Like many countries in South and Southeast Asia, dengue transmission in India is no longer confined to monsoon season. Several states report sustained year-round dengue transmissions expanding across both urban and rural areas. Reported cases exceeded 233,000 in 2024, but modeling analyses suggest the dengue burden in India appears to be higher than reported, likely representing tens of millions of infections each year.[2,3] With cases rising more than 11-fold in the past two decades and outbreaks growing more frequent and harder to predict, dengue is no longer a sporadic public health threat - it's an accelerating global challenge. "Dengue is a growing public health challenge, and India needs sustained, evidence-based prevention. The latest seven-year data for TAK-003 shows continued protection against dengue infection and hospitalization across all four serotypes; an important milestone for communities and health systems. Since its launch in 2022, TAK-003 has been approved in 43 countries, with more than 32 million doses distributed globally. This approval marks an important step forward in strengthening dengue prevention in India. Building on Takeda's 245-year heritage and more than 70 years of vaccine expertise, we remain committed to working with public health authorities, healthcare professionals, and partners to support equitable and sustainable access," said Dr. Mahender Nayak, Head of Intercontinental Markets, Takeda. TAK-003's approval in India is supported by an extensive global clinical development program, including a local Phase 3 study as well as data from the pivotal Phase 3 Tetravalent Immunization against Dengue Efficacy Study (TIDES), the largest and longest efficacy trial conducted for a dengue vaccine. In the TIDES trial, TAK-003 demonstrated sustained protection against dengue and dengue‐related hospitalization and a favorable safety profile. "Dengue is a shared regional challenge that requires collaboration across borders and sectors. Takeda is committed to working with governments, public health authorities, healthcare professionals and partners across South and Southeast Asia, not only to support access to TAK-003, where approved and recommended, but also to strengthen broader dengue prevention efforts, including surveillance, awareness, vector control and community engagement. Our aim is to contribute to sustainable, evidence-based solutions that reflect the needs of each country," said Peter Streibl, Cluster General Manager, Southeast Asia and India, Takeda. Since 2023, WHO's Strategic Advisory Group of Experts on Immunization (SAGE) endorsed the use of TAK-003 without the need for pre-vaccination screening.[4] Since then, TAK-003 has been recommended by the WHO for use in dengue-endemic settings, supporting its introduction in public immunization programs in areas with high transmission.[4] The vaccine has also received WHO prequalification, confirming it meets global standards for quality, safety and efficacy, and enabling eligibility for procurement through international agencies such as UNICEF and PAHO to support wider access.[5] Following the approval by DCGI, Takeda will continue to collaborate with Indian regulatory authorities, public health stakeholders, and healthcare providers to support the planning, delivery, and availability, of TAK-003 in India. In addition to India, TAK-003 is also approved in Indonesia, Thailand, Malaysia and Vietnam across the region. To date, TAK-003 has been distributed worldwide through public and private programs across Asia, Latin America and Europe, including Brazil's National Immunization Program and public programs in Argentina, Colombia and Indonesia. TAK-003 (Dengue Tetravalent Vaccine [Live, Attenuated]) TAK-003 is a dengue vaccine that is based on a live-attenuated dengue serotype 2 virus, which provides the genetic "backbone" for all four dengue virus serotypes and is designed to help protect against any of these serotypes.[6] Please consult with your local regulatory agency for approved labeling in your country. Please consult with local healthcare professionals for information regarding the vaccine. The drug information contained herein is intended to disclose corporate information. Nothing contained in this document should be considered a solicitation, promotion, or indication for any prescription drug, including those currently under development. 气候变化正在推动登革热在低收入和中等收入国家的传播

Climate change is driving dengue spread in low- and middle-income countries
新闻简报《PLOS 被忽视的热带病》中发表的一项系统综述表明,气候变化和快速城市化正在显着改变低收入和中等收入国家的登革热 传播。乔治城大学的研究人员分析了 2020 年至 2024 年间发表的 65 项研究,发现 65% 的研究将气温上升与疾病风险增加联系起来。气候变暖与病毒复制加速、蚊子叮咬率升高以及 传播 季节延长有关。此外,超过三分之一的研究将城市化因素(例如无计划的增长和卫生设施不足)与更高的登革热风险联系起来。该审查强调了登革热在以前低风险的高原和温带地区的出现。为了减轻这些风险,作者强调需要改进预警系统、气候知情的规划和基础设施投资。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表日期为 2026 年 7 月 22 日。系统评价分析了 2020 年至 2024 年发表的研究。
地点与对象
低收入和中等收入国家。提到的具体地区包括登革热流行的高原和温带地区。
事件
乔治城大学研究人员在公共科学图书馆《被忽视的热带病》上发表的一篇系统综述发现,气候变化和快速城市化正在重塑登革热 传播 模式。
规模、影响与风险
百分之六十五的审查研究将气温升高与疾病风险增加联系起来。气候变暖与病毒复制加速、蚊子叮咬率升高以及 传播 季节延长有关。登革热正在扩展到新的地区,包括以前的低风险或未受影响的地区。
应对、状态与不确定性
作者建议建立更好的早期预警系统、基于气候的 媒介 传播疾病规划以及对城市基础设施的投资,以应对不断变化的 媒介 模式。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Climate change is driving dengue spread in low- and middle-income countries

News Brief
A systematic review published in PLOS Neglected Tropical Diseases indicates that climate change and rapid urbanization are significantly altering dengue transmission in low- and middle-income countries. Researchers from Georgetown University analyzed 65 studies published between 2020 and 2024, finding that 65% linked rising temperatures to increased disease risk. Warmer conditions were associated with accelerated viral replication, higher mosquito biting rates, and extended transmission seasons. Additionally, over one-third of the studies connected urbanization factors, such as unplanned growth and inadequate sanitation, to higher dengue risk. The review highlights the emergence of dengue in previously low-risk highland and temperate regions. To mitigate these risks, the authors emphasize the need for improved early warning systems, climate-informed planning, and infrastructure investments.
Original source text (English)

Sixty-five percent of the studies tied rising temperatures to increased disease risk. “Warmer conditions were associated with accelerated viral replication, higher mosquito biting rates, and extended transmission seasons,” write the researchers. Urbanization, deforestation enable mosquito spreadMore than one-third of the studies linked urbanization to higher dengue risk. Many of those studies “identified unplanned urban growth, population density, and inadequate water or sanitation infrastructure as critical enablers of mosquito breeding and sustained transmission,” the researchers note. Urban heat islands, deforestation, and the expansion of urban areas were also found to amplify transmission risk.Several studies reported the emergence of dengue in areas that were previously considered low risk or unaffected.In addition, the findings suggest that dengue is expanding into new areas. “Several studies reported the emergence of dengue in areas that were previously considered low risk or unaffected, including highland and temperate regions, as rising temperatures enabled mosquito vectors to survive in new environments,” the authors write. Although some countries have adopted innovative response strategies, the authors say better early warning systems, climate-informed vector-borne disease planning, and investments in urban infrastructure are needed as climate change reshapes dengue’s transmission patterns. Some women experienced abnormal uterine bleeding earlier in COVID pandemic News brief Meghan Holohan Topics COVID-19 SewcreamStudio / iStock Earlier in the COVID-19 pandemic, some women reported menstrual periods that arrived early or late or spotting between periods, and now a study published yesterday in BMC Infectious Diseases has confirmed those reports.Abnormal uterine bleeding (AUB) was “reported by nearly half of women, regardless of HIV status,” wrote the authors, led by researchers at the University of Miami. “This AUB prevalence is higher than has previously been reported before the pandemic... which is especially notable because we excluded women who had many known causes of AUB.” Role of COVID-19, stressResearchers examined data from 188 women ages 18 to 45 who lived in the southern United States and didn’t have conditions that affect uterine bleeding (eg, polyendocrine metabolic ovarian syndrome (PMOS), endometriosis) to understand how COVID contributed to AUB. The women, 61% of whom had HIV, were in the Study of Treatment and Reproductive Outcomes (STAR). They answered questions about COVID-19 testing and vaccination, changes to their periods, stress, and demographic information. This AUB prevalence is higher than has previously been reported before the pandemic... which is especially notable because we excluded women who had many known causes of AUB.Of the women, 69% had a previous COVID-19 infection. AUB occurred more often in COVID-19 survivors who didn’t have HIV. Those who experienced more stress also had higher rates of AUB, although unusual bleeding occurred even in women who never had COVID-19. “Our study was conducted during the COVID-19 pandemic, when women may have been paying greater attention to menstrual patterns due to reports of AUB in the media, and women may have experienced other factors that could impact psychosocial factors, such as stress and AUB, such as food insecurity or sleep disruptions,” the authors wrote. The changes in uterine bleeding weren’t considered harmful, but the study’s findings may mean that another novel viral infection could also affect menstrual patterns, they added. New York City officials announce another death from Legionnaires’ disease News brief Stephanie Soucheray, MA Topics Legionella Drazen Zigic / iStock A fifth person has died from Legionnaires’ disease in an outbreak in Manhattan, New York officials said late last night. The outbreak has been connected to bacteria found in cooling towers on the Upper East Side. So far, 82 cases of Legionnaires’ disease have been identified, and eight people remain hospitalized. Fifty-six people have been discharged from the hospital, and 13 were never hospitalized. Officials have tested 183 cooling towers at 160 buildings, and 77 cooling towers were positive for Legionella bacteria. “Legionnaires' disease is not caused by any building's plumbing system,” NYC Health said. “Residents in ZIP codes 10028, 10128, and 10075 can continue to drink tap water, bathe, shower, cook, and use your air conditioner at home.”About 10% of people with Legionnaires’ disease die from their infections. Early treatment with antibiotics can help limit disease severity. Women have worse signs, symptoms of neurologic long COVID than men, researchers say News brief Mary Van Beusekom, MS Topics COVID-19 Nattakorn Maneerat / iStock More than a year after COVID-19 symptom onset, both previously hospitalized and nonhospitalized women who had long COVID reported four neurologic symptoms more often than their male peers, Northwestern University researchers write.For the study, published yesterday in the Annals of Clinical and Translational Neurology, the authors analyzed 261 hospitalized patients (PNP) and 2,068 nonhospitalized patients (NNP) who visited a long-COVID clinic from May 2020 to August 2025.“While it has long been known that PASC [post-acute sequelae of SARS-CoV-2, or long COVID] more frequently affects women than men, whether an association exists between sex and Neuro-PASC has not been determined in detail,” they wrote. More-severe fatigue, less ability to focusAn average of 16 months after COVID symptoms began, women in both groups reported four neurologic symptoms more often than men (PNP, 79.6% vs 64.9%; NNP, 76.1% vs 64.6%). Both female PNPs and NNPs more often cited numbness/tingling, dizziness, headache, and pain than men, but only female NNPs reported brain fog, muscle pain, and impaired sense of taste and smell more often than male NNPs.Females suffer from a higher symptom burden of Neuro-PASC and had worse subjective quality of life and objective cognitive function.Women in both groups also more frequently reported fatigue, shortness of breath, chest pain, and dysautonomia (a nervous system disorder) than men, while only female NNPs cited more frequent gastrointestinal symptoms than male NNPs. Relative to men, women from both groups indicated worse fatigue on the Patient-Reported Outcome Measure Information System questionnaire, and female NNPs performed worse on a task of attention on the National Institutes of Health Toolbox cognitive test.“Females suffer from a higher symptom burden of Neuro-PASC and had worse subjective quality of life and objective cognitive function,” the researchers wrote. “These results highlight the importance of timely diagnosis and targeted interventions in female patients who are often negatively affected by gender bias in medicine.” In case you missed it Previous Next Quick takes: Africa CDC head asks RFK to lift Uganda travel ban, Pennsylvania tracks growing measles outbreak, 2 more Texas counties detect New World screwworm Preemptive prescribing of an antibiotic significantly reduced STI rates at a San Francisco clinic. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

Qdenga 登革热 疫苗 在印度获批 - Kiddaan

Qdenga Dengue Vaccine Approved in India - Kiddaan
新闻简报India has approved its first dengue vaccine, Qdenga (TAK-003), developed by Takeda Biopharmaceuticals, marking a significant public health milestone. The Drug Controller General of India (DCGI) authorized the two-dose vaccine for individuals aged four to 60, regardless of prior dengue infection, eliminating the need for pre-vaccination screening. The approval is supported by clinical trials involving over 28,000 participants, which showed 80.2% overall efficacy against virologically confirmed dengue and sustained protection against hospitalization. With India bearing nearly one-third of the global dengue burden, this vaccine is expected to reduce severe infections. Takeda partnered with Biological E to manufacture up to 50 million doses annually in Hyderabad, potentially enhancing affordability and availability, though pricing details remain pending.
查看新闻五要素
时间
该批准报告于2026年7月20日公布。
地点与对象
该批准由印度药品监管总局 (DCGI) 批准,适用于印度人口,特别针对 4 至 60 岁的个人。
事件
印度药品监管总局 (DCGI) 批准武田生物制药印度公司的 Qdenga 登革热 疫苗 (TAK-003),这是该国首个获批的登革热 疫苗。
规模、影响与风险
印度占全球登革热负担的近三分之一,报告的病例在过去二十年大幅增加。临床试验表明,在第二次给药后 12 个月,对病毒学确诊的登革热的总体疗效为 80.2%,并提供了针对登革热相关住院治疗的持续保护。
应对、状态与不确定性
Takeda has partnered with Biological E in Hyderabad to manufacture up to 50 million doses annually. While the price has not been announced, local production is expected to increase affordability. The vaccine does not require pre-vaccination screening and is administered in two doses three months apart.
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Qdenga Dengue Vaccine Approved in India - Kiddaan

News Brief
India has approved its first dengue vaccine, Qdenga (TAK-003), developed by Takeda Biopharmaceuticals, marking a significant public health milestone. The Drug Controller General of India (DCGI) authorized the two-dose vaccine for individuals aged four to 60, regardless of prior dengue infection, eliminating the need for pre-vaccination screening. The approval is supported by clinical trials involving over 28,000 participants, which showed 80.2% overall efficacy against virologically confirmed dengue and sustained protection against hospitalization. With India bearing nearly one-third of the global dengue burden, this vaccine is expected to reduce severe infections. Takeda partnered with Biological E to manufacture up to 50 million doses annually in Hyderabad, potentially enhancing affordability and availability, though pricing details remain pending.
Original source text (English)
India has approved the Qdenga dengue vaccine, making it the country’s first approved dengue vaccine. The Drug Controller General of India (DCGI) granted approval to Takeda Biopharmaceuticals India’s vaccine, which is designed to protect against all four dengue virus strains. The approval comes at a time when dengue cases are rising steadily across the country, making the Qdenga dengue vaccine a significant public health milestone. What is the Qdenga dengue vaccine? The Qdenga dengue vaccine is designed to provide protection against all four dengue virus serotypes—DENV-1, DENV-2, DENV-3 and DENV-4. It has been approved for people aged between four and 60 years, regardless of whether they have previously had dengue. One of its key advantages is that it does not require pre-vaccination screening. The vaccine is administered through two subcutaneous doses of 0.5 mL each, with the second dose given three months after the first. What did the clinical trials find? The approval is based on Takeda’s global clinical development programme, which included 19 Phase 1, 2 and 3 clinical trials involving more than 28,000 participants across dengue-endemic and non-endemic regions. Its Phase III TIDES (DEN-301) trial studied over 20,000 participants in eight countries and assessed the vaccine’s long-term safety and effectiveness. The findings showed that the Qdenga dengue vaccine achieved an overall efficacy of 80.2% against virologically confirmed dengue 12 months after the second dose. The study also found that it offered sustained protection against dengue-related hospitalisation. India has approved QDENGA (TAK-003) as the country's first dengue vaccine, marking a major step in the fight against one of India's most common mosquito-borne diseases. pic.twitter.com/A86TJC2F3g — Firstpost (@firstpost) July 20, 2026 When will it be available? Takeda has partnered with Hyderabad-based Biological E to manufacture up to 50 million doses annually in India. While the company has not yet announced the vaccine’s price, local production is expected to make it more affordable. The final cost may also depend on whether the vaccine is included in future government immunisation programmes. India accounts for nearly one-third of the global dengue burden, with reported cases increasing significantly over the past two decades. Health experts believe the Qdenga dengue vaccine could play an important role in reducing severe infections and dengue-related hospitalisations. FAQs Q: What is the Qdenga dengue vaccine? A: The Qdenga dengue vaccine is India’s first approved dengue vaccine and protects against all four dengue virus strains. Q: Who can receive the Qdenga dengue vaccine? A: It is approved for individuals aged 4 to 60 years, regardless of previous dengue infection, without requiring pre-vaccination testing. Q: How many doses are required? A: The vaccine is given in two doses of 0.5 mL each, administered three months apart. Q: How effective is the vaccine? A: Clinical trials showed the vaccine had an overall efficacy of 80.2% against virologically confirmed dengue one year after the second dose

武田的 QDENGA,印度首个获批的登革热 疫苗 - NewsBricks

Takeda's QDENGA, India's First Approved Dengue Vaccine - NewsBricks
新闻简报印度已批准武田的 QDENGA (TAK-003),这是其首个用于 登革热 的 疫苗,针对 4 至 60 岁的个体。经印度药品监管总局批准,两剂四价 疫苗 可解决所有四种 登革病毒 血清型,不需要 疫苗接种 前筛查。涉及超过 28,000 名参与者的临床试验显示,疫苗接种 治疗一年后,对症状性登革热的疗效为 80.2%,对 18 个月住院治疗的疗效为 90.4%。这项批准是在印度报告的登革热感染病例在二十年来增加了十一倍多之际发布的。虽然预计到 2027 年上半年将在私营部门实现商业化,但公共卫生专家坚持认为,疫苗接种 必须补充而不是取代现有的蚊虫控制措施,以有效管理疾病负担。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 21 日。预计将于 2027 年上半年在印度私营医疗保健行业实现商业化。
地点与对象
疫苗 批准用于 4 至 60 岁的个人。
事件
印度药品监管总局 (DCGI) 批准了武田的 QDENGA (TAK-003),这是该国首个获批的登革热 疫苗。它是一种四价 疫苗,针对所有四种 登革病毒 血清型。
规模、影响与风险
过去二十年来,印度报告的登革热感染人数增加了十一倍多。疫苗 在第二次给药一年后证明,在预防确诊症状登革热病例方面有 80.2% 的功效,在 疫苗接种 后 18 个月评估,在预防登革热相关住院方面的功效为 90.4%。
应对、状态与不确定性
疫苗 进行两次皮下注射,间隔三个月,不需要 疫苗接种 前血液测试。专家强调,仅靠 疫苗接种 并不能消除登革热,仍然需要继续采取蚊虫控制措施。预计将进行长期安全监测和持续的 监测。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Takeda's QDENGA, India's First Approved Dengue Vaccine - NewsBricks

News Brief
India has approved Takeda's QDENGA (TAK-003), its first vaccine for dengue fever, targeting individuals aged 4 to 60. Approved by the Drug Controller General of India, the two-dose tetravalent vaccine addresses all four dengue virus serotypes and does not require pre-vaccination screening. Clinical trials involving over 28,000 participants showed 80.2% efficacy against symptomatic dengue one year post-vaccination and 90.4% efficacy against hospitalization at 18 months. This approval comes as reported dengue infections in India have risen more than eleven-fold in two decades. While commercial availability in the private sector is expected by the first half of 2027, public health experts maintain that vaccination must complement, not replace, existing mosquito control measures to effectively manage the disease burden.
Original source text (English)
Takeda's QDENGA, India approves the Dengue Vaccine, Here's what you need to know India has approved Takeda's QDENGA (TAK-003), the country's first dengue vaccine for people aged 4 to 60. The two-dose vaccine targets all four dengue virus strains and has shown strong efficacy in reducing symptomatic infections,hospitalisations, marking a major step in India's fight against dengue India has approved QDENGA (TAK-003), developed by Japanese pharmaceutical company Takeda, making it the country's first approved vaccine against dengue fever. The approval by the Drug Controller General of India (DCGI) is considered a landmark step in India's fight against one of its mosquito-borne diseases. The vaccine is approved for individuals aged 4 to 60 and can be administered regardless of whether they have been affected by dengue previously. EducationalLMS platform Advertisement Discover more History Celebrities & Entertainment News vaccines Recently, India has continued to witness a sharp increase in dengue cases due to rapid urbanisation, climate change, longer mosquito breeding seasons, and population growth. According to available data, reported dengue infections in India have increased more than eleven-fold over the past two decades. This highlights the urgent need for better preventive measures beyond mosquito control alone. MedicalLiterature & Resources What Is Dengue? Dengue is a viral disease transmitted through the bite of infected Aedes aegypti and Aedes albopictus mosquitoes, which are active only during the daytime. It is caused by four closely related but distinct virus serotypes: DENV-1 DENV-2 DENV-3 DENV-4 An infection with only one serotype can cause lifelong immunity only against that specific strain. A person can still be affected by the other three serotypes later in life; in fact, a second infection caused by another serotype will increase the risk of severe dengue because of a phenomenon known as antibody-dependent enhancement (ADE), and this may also lead to death. InfectiousDiseases Symptoms of dengue appear 4 to 10 days after a mosquito bite and include: High fever Severe headache Pain behind the eyes Muscle and joint pain Skin rash Nausea and vomiting While many people recover within a week, some may develop severe dengue, which is called hemorrhagic fever or dengue shock syndrome, which can cause plasma leakage, organ failure, severe bleeding and even death if not treated properly. What is QDENGA? QDENGA, scientifically known as TAK-003, is a live-attenuated tetravalent dengue vaccine, meaning it contains weakened forms of the virus that do not cause normal dengue illnesses but stimulate the body's immune system to produce protective antibodies. GeographicReference Tetravalent indicates that the vaccine is designed to treat all four dengue virus serotypes, making it one of the most comprehensive dengue vaccines currently available. Unlike the earlier vaccine Dengvaxia, QDENGA does not require pre-vaccination blood testing to confirm whether the person has been affected by dengue before. This simplifies the vaccination programs and makes the vaccine easier to use in countries where large-scale screening would be difficult. Dengue is administered as: Two subcutaneous injections 0.5 mL per dose Three months apart Efficacy And Safety Performance Takeda conducted a dengue vaccine development program in which more than 28,000 participants were included, and 19 Phase I, II, and III clinical trials were conducted. Pharmaceuticals& Biotech In the Phase III TIDES (DEN-301) trials, more than 20,000 children and adolescents from eight countries were enrolled. The clinical trials show an 80.2% efficacy in preventing confirmed symptomatic dengue cases one year after the second dose. It demonstrates 90.4% efficacy against dengue-related hospitalisation, evaluated 18 months post-vaccination. Long-term data from its global TIDES clinical trial show protection extending up to 7 years. The common side-effects that would appear in the person who takes the vaccine include: Pain/redness in the injected site Headache Muscle pain Mild fever And as the vaccine is live-attenuated, it must not be given to pregnant or breastfeeding women or individuals with a compromised immune system. Vaccines& Immunizations Why Does India Need a Dengue Vaccine Now? India carries one of the world's largest dengue burdens; traditional prevention strategies include fogging, larvicide use, eliminating stagnant water, and public awareness campaigns, which have helped but have not prevented recurring outbreaks. And there is no specific antiviral treatment for dengue; patient care mainly focuses on supportive hydration, monitoring, and management of complications. Preventive measures remain one of the most effective strategies for reducing illness and deaths. Public health experts believe that vaccination could significantly reduce the rising number of severe cases requiring hospitalisation, easing pressure on the healthcare system during seasonal outbreaks. Will the Vaccine End Dengue? Experts warn that vaccination alone will not eliminate dengue; mosquitoes will continue to transmit the virus unless control measures remain effective. HealthConditions People will still need to: Prevent stagnant water accumulation Use mosquito repellents Install window screens and mosquito nets where appropriate Wear long-sleeved clothing Seek early medical care if symptoms develop Instead of eliminating dengue, QDENGA is expected to reduce severe illness, hospitalisations and deaths, particularly in regions where dengue is widespread. QDENGA is approved by 40 other countries, including the European Union, the UK, Brazil, Indonesia, and Argentina. It has also achieved the official World Health Organisation (WHO) pre-qualification. Commercial availability in India's private healthcare sector is anticipated by the first half of 2027. EducationalLMS platform India's approval of Takeda's QDENGA represents one of the country's most important advances in dengue prevention. Although continued surveillance and long-term safety monitoring will be important, experts believe that the vaccine has the potential to reduce the burden of severe dengue and protect millions of people living in endemic regions. 登革病毒3型(DENV-3)3III_B.3.2谱系在巴西的分子特征与近期传播:蛋白基序与流行病学的信息学(in silico)分析 - 生物通

News article (Chinese original; verified English title unavailable)
新闻简报发表在 Virus Genes 上的一项研究检查了巴西 登革病毒 3 型 (DENV-3) 的分子特征和近期流行病学趋势,重点关注 3III_B.3.2 谱系的出现。通过分析2013年至2025年12月的数据,研究人员发现,虽然DENV-1在历史上占主导地位,但DENV-3病例在2024年和2025年显着激增,到2025年12月记录了6,766例。2022年左右引入的3III_B.3.2谱系已经取代了以前的谱系,并与可能增强病毒复制的特定氨基酸基序删除有关。该研究利用公共数据库中的基因组序列来识别这些变化,并指出该谱系在先前对 DENV-3 免疫力较低的人群中迅速传播。研究人员强调需要进一步进行体内研究,以验证这些分子变化对病毒适应性和 传播 的功能影响。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究分析了 2013 年至 2025 年 12 月的数据,特别关注 2022 年左右 3III_B.3.2 谱系的引入及其在 2023 年至 2025 年的传播。发布日期为 2026 年 7 月 20 日。
地点与对象
巴西,特别提到东南地区(例如米纳斯吉拉斯州)作为高 发病率 区域。受影响人群包括20-39岁的人群,其次是40-59岁和15-19岁的人群。
事件
发表在 Virus Genes 上的一项生物信息学研究描述了 DENV-3 谱系 3III_B.3.2 的分子特征和近期在巴西的传播。该研究发现了与该谱系相关的特定氨基酸基序缺失,它取代了之前流行的谱系(3III_C.2 和 3III_C.2.2),并与 DENV-3 病例的增加相关。
规模、影响与风险
2014年至2025年12月期间,巴西记录了15,970,928例确诊的登革热病例,2024年达到峰值5,988,245例。DENV-3病例从2023年的106例大幅增加到2024年的1,792例,到2025年12月达到6,766例。3III_B.3.2谱系与复制增加有关成功和流行病学传播。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究利用公共数据库(DATASUS/TABNET、GenBank、GISAID)和计算工具(MAFFT、Nextclade、IQ-Tree)进行分析。研究人员指出,由于测序工作不均匀和对计算机预测的依赖,采样偏差存在局限性,建议未来进行体内研究以确认蛋白质基序的功能影响。

News article (Chinese original; verified English title unavailable)

Original title (Chinese)
登革病毒3型(DENV-3)3III_B.3.2谱系在巴西的分子特征与近期传播:蛋白基序与流行病学的信息学(in silico)分析 - 生物通
News Brief
A study published in Virus Genes examined the molecular characteristics and recent epidemiological trends of Dengue virus type 3 (DENV-3) in Brazil, focusing on the emergence of the 3III_B.3.2 lineage. Analyzing data from 2013 to December 2025, researchers found that while DENV-1 was historically dominant, DENV-3 cases surged significantly in 2024 and 2025, with 6,766 cases recorded by December 2025. The 3III_B.3.2 lineage, introduced around 2022, has replaced previous lineages and is linked to specific amino acid motif deletions that may enhance viral replication. The study utilized genomic sequences from public databases to identify these changes, noting the lineage's rapid spread amidst a population with low prior immunity to DENV-3. Researchers highlighted the need for further in vivo studies to validate the functional impact of these molecular changes on viral fitness and transmission.
Original source text (Chinese)

“畅读会员”点此登录查看原文链接Original Paper Open access 登革病毒(Dengue virus, DENV)属于黄病毒科(Flaviviridae)家族,是一种具有全球相关性的发热性虫媒病毒疾病病原体。在巴西,自上世纪以来四种病毒血清型呈地方性(endemic)循环,其中DENV-3常关联更严重的临床状况与重大流行。本研究旨在对巴西循环的DENV-3毒株进行基序(motif)表征及其流行病学分析。研究人员从DATASUS/TABNET获取2013年至2025年按血清型分列的病例信息。此外,分析了GenBank与GISAID数据库中可用的184条巴西样本完整基因组,以DENV-3参考基因组序列(NC_001475.2)为参照。序列使用MAFFT、BioEdit、Nextclade及GeneDoc软件进行编辑与分析以识别谱系与氨基酸基序,随后提交至IQ-Tree与FigTree程序进行系统发育分析。结果表明历史上血清型1占主导,但2024年DENV-2与DENV-3病例较上一年显著增加。截至2025年12月,当年DENV-3病例数已超越DENV-1。全基因组共鉴定出九个氨基酸基序,特别是N-豆蔻酰化(N-myristoylation)、酪蛋白激酶II(Casein kinase II)磷酸化、蛋白激酶C(Protein kinase C)磷酸化及N-糖基化(N-glycosylation)位点。2022年前主导谱系为3III_C.2与3III_C.2.2,彼时谱系3III_B.3.2被引入巴西,其与该病例增加相关。该基因型相较于先前谱系呈现取代及蛋白基序缺失,提示此类改变促成了其复制成功。研究背景方面,登革热是由登革病毒(DENV,黄病毒科Flaviviridae成员)引起、经埃及伊蚊(Aedes aegypti)或白纹伊蚊(A. albopictus)传播的动物源性发热疾病,其基因组约为一万一千个核苷酸的正链单股RNA,编码三种结构蛋白(C、M、E)与七种非结构(NS)蛋白(NS1、NS2A、NS2B、NS3、NS4A、NS4B、NS5),其中DENV-2与DENV-3常关联更严重病例。巴西自上世纪起四种血清型呈地方性循环,1980年代首次在罗赖马州记录DENV-1与DENV-4流行,2000年代起DENV-2与DENV-3出现增加了流行病学复杂性,热带亚热带气候、无序城市化与全球化助推了疫情。2025年2月泛美卫生组织(PAHO)就美洲DENV-3循环发出警报,指出人群因缺乏既往暴露而广泛易感且DENV-3可致重症;2024年血清型3重新兴起于巴西东南部并关联重症,而历史上DENV-1在2023年最常见,但2024年DENV-2与DENV-3显著增加,2025年12月DENV-3病例已超DENV-1。当前控制依赖灭蚊与疫苗接种(如Dengvaxia?、Qdenga?),但人口流动、气候变化与增长仍致高发,因此研究人员旨在对巴西历年DENV-3毒株进行分子表征并评估鉴定巴西样本氨基酸序列中的结构基序突变。下载激活素(Activins)海报获取相关药物开发信息。激活素和抑制素是治疗生殖系统疾病的潜在靶点。领 取 研究人员开展的研究基于2013年至2025年12月DATASUS/TABNET报告的巴西各州实验室确诊或临床流行病学诊断的登革病例数据,以及从GISAID与NCBI数据库筛选的184条巴西DENV-3完整编码序列(过滤条件为完整编码DNA序列、高覆盖度、完整采集日期、巴西地点、无缺失基因且长度≥8000核苷酸),以NC_001475.2为参考,使用MAFFT在线服务器比对,BioEdit v7.2.5校正读码框与移码,GeneDoc v2.7.000基于PROSITE模式识别蛋白基序与保守区,Nextclade v3.18.1进行谱系分类与质量评分,IQ-Tree在线服务器选用TIM2+F+I+G4模型并以1000次SH-aLRT与UFBoot进行最大似然(Maximum Likelihood)系统发育分析,FigTree v1.4.4观察系统发生树并以NC_001475.2重根。 研究结果部分,首先是病例数(Number of cases):根据DATASUS数据,2014年至2025年12月巴西累计记录15,970,928例确诊登革病例,2024年为峰值(5,988,245例),东南部最高发,其次为南部、中西部、东北部与北部,主要受累人群为20–39岁,其次40–59岁与15–19岁;可分血清型中共343,139例,DENV-1占215,066例(62.67%),其次DENV-2(34.27%)、DENO-3(2.56%)、DENV-4(0.50%);2024年DENV-3感染1,792例较2023年(106例)显著增加,2025年12月达6,766例超上年总量。 其次是DENV-3数据集分析(Analysis of the DENV-3 dataset):基于参考基因组的184条序列多重比对识别出九个不同蛋白基序,其中酪蛋白激酶II(CK2)基序54次(19次在NS5内),蛋白激酶C(PKC)位点39次(9次在NS3),cAMP依赖性蛋白激酶(PKA)位点10次(3次在锚定衣壳ancC蛋白),酪氨酸激酶磷酸化位点仅6次;N-豆蔻酰化位点55次(E与NS3各13次);N-糖基化位点12个(E、NS5各3个,NS4B 2个,prM/pr、NS1、NS2A、NS3各1个)。Nextclade谱系分配显示参考序列属3III_A谱系;数据集中3III_B.3.2最常见(N=95),其次3III_C.2(N=58)、3III_C.2.2(N=28)及2003年单条3III_C.1;2014年前样本多属3III_C(除3V基因型),序列高度保守且共享多数基序。三条例外基因组(EPI_ISL_19179567等)在NS2A第1206aa处提前终止密码子致77.5%截断,Nextclade评为“mediocre”且质量分约61.1,属测序组装假象非生物学变异。最近序列NS5第2710aa处基序缺失可能源于2022年起新循环基因型,3III_B谱系具此改变且2024年米纳斯吉拉斯州一例同谱系的也缺失;第1433aa基序可能与宿主或局部循环有关。最大似然系统发育树显示两主要分支:基部分支(SH-aLRT/UFboot 94.9/100)聚集3III_C.1(N=1)、3III_C.2与3III_C.2.2(2001–2013年多区域);2006与2007年两例3V基因型位于基部外支未分组,其差异为第691、1144、2072aa无N-豆蔻酰化,第944aa有CK2磷酸化,第1270与3105aa有肌酸磷酸激酶磷酸化,第2810aa无N-糖基化;上部分支为2023–2025年全国的3III_B.3.2谱系(分支支持100/100),暗示其进化与扩散成功。 讨论部分总结:比对中磷酸化基序多见于DENV-3非结构蛋白,或助病毒生物合成与免疫逃逸;PKC磷酸化位点在NS3与NS1较多(调控复制与多蛋白切割),PKC抑制反增复制;10个PKA磷酸化基序中3个在ancC,或改善C蛋白信号与切割以增强装配;CK2位点在NS5最多(19个),NS5有胞质(与NS3互作)与核型,DENV-2中NS5苏氨酸395的CK2磷酸化减核转运碍复制,丙肝病毒NS5A此磷酸化对复制必需,故推测这些基序对DENV-3复制成功有复杂调控。N-豆蔻酰化在E、NS5、NS3高频,或助靶细胞进入与复制复合物稳定(如DENV-2中NS3重分布脂肪酸合酶);12个N-糖基化位点中E蛋白通常两个,糖基化后与prM互作折叠提毒力与感染性,NS1的N-糖基化稳蛋白促病毒出胞,这些基序参与病毒成熟与出胞。3III基因型自2000年代初巴西首疫来致最多病例,源于1960年代印度、1980年代斯里兰卡与非洲、1990年代拉美,从加勒比、哥伦比亚、委内瑞拉入巴西并区域多样化形成亚支;数据中3III_C.2、3III_C.2.2与3III_B.3.2为主,后者2023–2025出现印证2022年引入巴西;DENV-3超十五年低循环致人群免疫缺口(PAHO警示),3III_B.3.2在此易感背景下快速扩散反映进化轨迹与流行潜力。3V基因型分类曾争议(Lanciotti等归3I亚组,Nogueira等与Araújo等证其与亚洲1956–1980序列聚且不近他型而独立)。 结论部分翻译:在这项工作中,研究人员观察了巴西DENV-3的流行病学演变及该国分离序列中的结构基序。2022年3III_B.3.2谱系的出现导致DENV-3病例上升,引发流行担忧。该谱系与此前国内循环的谱系(3III_C.2与3III_C.2.2)特征相似,基序差异极少,除第1861aa与第2710aa位置外,这提示这些改变有利于该病毒在巴西的增殖。尽管如此,本研究受限于公共数据库沉积的样本、序列质量及所用工具;基因组监测完全依赖公共数据库沉积序列,可能因不同地区测序努力差异引入地理与时间采样偏差,潜在低估某些区域或暴发早期阶段;此外本研究为预测性表征且仅用计算分析,未来应进行体内研究以理解各血清型与基因型的特性并分析蛋白基序与结构对循环基因型的影响,从而预测与预防巴西DENV-3新流行。论文发表于《Virus View five news elements
Time
The study analyzes data from 2013 to December 2025, with specific focus on the introduction of the 3III_B.3.2 lineage around 2022 and its spread through 2023–2025. The publication date is July 20, 2026.
Location and population
Brazil, with specific mention of the Southeast region (e.g., Minas Gerais state) as a high-incidence area. The affected population includes individuals aged 20–39, followed by those aged 40–59 and 15–19.
Event
A bioinformatics study published in Virus Genes characterized the molecular features and recent spread of DENV-3 lineage 3III_B.3.2 in Brazil. The research identified specific amino acid motif deletions associated with this lineage, which replaced previously circulating lineages (3III_C.2 and 3III_C.2.2) and correlated with a rise in DENV-3 cases.
Scale, impact and risk
Brazil recorded 15,970,928 confirmed dengue cases between 2014 and December 2025, with a peak of 5,988,245 cases in 2024. DENV-3 cases rose significantly from 106 in 2023 to 1,792 in 2024, reaching 6,766 by December 2025. The 3III_B.3.2 lineage is associated with increased replication success and epidemiological spread.
Response, status and uncertainty
The study utilized public databases (DATASUS/TABNET, GenBank, GISAID) and computational tools (MAFFT, Nextclade, IQ-Tree) for analysis. Researchers noted limitations regarding sampling biases due to uneven sequencing efforts and the reliance on in silico predictions, recommending future in vivo studies to confirm the functional impact of protein motifs.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

印度批准登革热 疫苗:医生解释四种病毒株以及为什么免疫仍然是一个重大挑战 - 印度时报

India approves dengue vaccine: Doctor explains the four virus strains and why immunity remains a major challenge - The Times of India
新闻简报印度已批准其首款登革热药物 疫苗 QDENGA (TAK-003),由印度武田生物制药公司开发,适用于 4 至 60 岁的个体。两剂方案间隔三个月,旨在预防所有四种 登革病毒 血清型(DENV-1 至 DENV-4),无需进行 疫苗接种 预筛查。虽然 疫苗 提供广泛的保护,旨在降低 重症登革热 和住院的风险,但专家指出,没有 疫苗 提供 100% 的免疫力。卫生当局建议,疫苗接种 应补充而不是替代蚊虫叮咬的预防措施,并应根据国家指南进行管理。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 20 日。
地点与对象
印度;符合新批准的 疫苗 资格的 4 至 60 岁人口。
事件
印度已批准其首款登革热药物 疫苗 QDENGA (TAK-003),由武田生物制药印度公司开发,用于 4 至 60 岁的个体。
规模、影响与风险
疫苗 旨在预防所有四种 登革病毒 血清型(DENV-1 至 DENV-4),旨在降低 重症登革热 和住院的风险,尽管它不能对每种感染提供 100% 的免疫力。
应对、状态与不确定性
疫苗 分两次给药,间隔三个月,并且不需要 疫苗接种 前筛查。专家强调,疫苗接种 应补充而不是替代蚊虫叮咬预防措施,并根据国家指南进行。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

India approves dengue vaccine: Doctor explains the four virus strains and why immunity remains a major challenge - The Times of India

News Brief
India has approved its first dengue vaccine, QDENGA (TAK-003), developed by Takeda Biopharmaceuticals India, for individuals aged 4 to 60 years. The two-dose regimen, spaced three months apart, is designed to protect against all four dengue virus serotypes (DENV-1 to DENV-4) without requiring pre-vaccination screening. While the vaccine offers broad protection and aims to reduce the risk of severe dengue and hospitalization, experts note that no vaccine provides 100% immunity. Health authorities advise that vaccination should complement, not replace, preventive measures against mosquito bites and should be administered in accordance with national guidelines.
Original source text (English)

ProtestCJP ProtestAbhijeet DipkeAll Comments (0)+0/3000Be the first one to review.NewsHealth NewsIndia approves dengue vaccine: Doctor explains the four virus strains and why immunity remains a major challengeIndia approves dengue vaccine: Doctor explains the four virus strains and why immunity remains a major challengeAadya Jha / TIMESOFINDIA.COM / Updated: Jul 20, 2026, 17:41 ISTShareAA+Text SizeSmallMediumLargeFollow us 1/8Why dengue immunity is more complicated than you thinkMonsoon often brings relief from the heat, but it also marks the return of one of India's biggest seasonal health concerns: dengue. Every year, thousands of people assume that once they have recovered from dengue, they have little to worry about in the future. It sounds logical, but unfortunately, that is not how dengue works.Unlike many viral infections that leave behind broad protection, dengue is far more complicated. The virus exists in four different forms, known as serotypes or strains. An infection caused by one strain usually protects a person only from that specific strain, leaving the door open for infection by the other three.Read More2/8India's first dengue vaccine: What you should knowIndia has approved its first dengue vaccine, QDENGA (TAK-003), developed by Takeda Biopharmaceuticals India, for people aged 4 to 60 years. Given in two doses, three months apart, the vaccine is designed to protect against all four dengue virus serotypes (DENV-1 to DENV-4) and does not require pre-vaccination screening. While it offers broad protection and reduces the risk of severe dengue and hospitalisation, no vaccine provides 100% immunity against every infection or strain. Experts say vaccination should be taken according to national guidelines and should complement, not replace, measures to prevent mosquito bites.Read More3/8Dengue is not one virus, it comes in four different serotypesThe dengue virus belongs to a family called flaviviruses and exists as four distinct serotypes: DENV-1, DENV-2, DENV-3, and DENV-4. Although they are closely related, they are different enough for the immune system to recognise them separately.Dr Raj Kumar, Sr. Consultant, Pulmonary Medicine, ISIC Multispeciality Hospital, explained, "Dengue continues to be one of the most prevalent mosquito-borne viral diseases, particularly in the monsoon months. What many are not aware of is that there are four virus strains (serotypes) of Dengue. Once infected with one strain, long-term immunity is typically only established against that one strain. They may still become infected with the remaining three strains in the future, though."This is why someone who had dengue five years ago is not automatically protected today. If another serotype is circulating in the community, a fresh infection remains possible.Read More4/8Why a second dengue infection can sometimes be more dangerousOne of the reasons doctors take repeat dengue infections seriously is a process called antibody-dependent enhancement (ADE). Scientists believe that antibodies formed during the first infection may not completely neutralise a different serotype during a later infection. Instead, they may unintentionally help the virus enter immune cells more efficiently, increasing the risk of severe disease in some people.Dr Kumar said, "As a matter of fact, dengue fever can be more serious upon a second infection with a different strain of the virus. That's why it's still the best way to avoid mosquito bites, even after experiencing dengue."This does not mean every second infection becomes severe. Many people recover completely. However, doctors recommend taking every episode of dengue seriously, regardless of whether someone has had the illness before.Read More5/8If there are four strains, can one vaccine really protect against all of them?This is where dengue vaccination becomes more complex than vaccines for many other viral diseases.An ideal dengue vaccine needs to generate strong and balanced protection against all four serotypes. That is why dengue vaccines have undergone extensive evaluation, and different countries recommend different vaccines for different groups of people.Dr Kumar explained, "There is also a lot of debate as to whether one dengue vaccine will be sufficient to protect against all strains. The solution is not as simple as it sounds. Complete protection is required from all four strains of the dengue virus in an ideal vaccine, as incomplete protection may not be adequate."He further added, "Currently, guidelines for the use of dengue vaccines differ from country to country and are based on age, prior dengue infection, disease burden, and which vaccine is approved. Thus, vaccination should only be done after consulting a competent health expert and following the national health guidelines."Read More6/8Even after vaccination, mosquito protection remains essentialDr Kumar said, "It is important to recognize that a vaccine is not 100% effective in eradicating the risk of dengue. Simple preventive measures are also as important."He advised, "Individuals should be protected from the bite of mosquitoes by using mosquito repellents, wearing long-sleeved clothes, using mosquito nets while sleeping, and ensuring that there is no stagnant water around the house where mosquitoes can breed."Sometimes, preventing dengue begins with something as simple as emptying a forgotten flower pot tray or covering a water storage container. These small habits can interrupt the breeding cycle of Aedes mosquitoes before they spread infection.Read More7/8Know the warning signs, and don't wait for symptoms to worsenDengue often starts with symptoms that resemble many other viral illnesses. But timely diagnosis can make a significant difference, especially when hydration and medical monitoring are started early.Dr Kumar said, "If they start to become ill with a high fever, a very bad headache, a headache behind the eyes, joint pain, nausea, vomiting or rashes, they should go to the doctor immediately. The earlier the diagnosis, the better to prevent complications when it comes to hydration and timely medical care."He added, "Dengue can be treated successfully if recognised in time, but if not, it can worsen to a serious illness."Read More8/8Medical experts consultedThis article includes expert inputs shared with TOI Health by:Dr Raj Kumar, Sr. Consultant, Pulmonary Medicine, ISIC Multispeciality Hospital.Inputs were used to explain why dengue infection does not provide protection against all virus strains, why repeat infections can still occur, and why vaccination should be taken only after consulting a qualified healthcare professional.Read MoreFollow Us On Social MediaPhotostoriesFrom having no work for three years after quitting Sasural Simar Ka to wife Dipika Kakar's financial support; When Shoaib Ibrahim spoke about his struggles, career and lifeIndia approves dengue vaccine: Doctor explains the four virus strains and why immunity remains a major challengeLoved 'The Odyssey'? Time
Published on July 20, 2026.
Location and population
India; population aged 4 to 60 years eligible for the newly approved vaccine.
Event
India has approved its first dengue vaccine, QDENGA (TAK-003), developed by Takeda Biopharmaceuticals India, for use in individuals aged 4 to 60 years.
Scale, impact and risk
The vaccine is designed to protect against all four dengue virus serotypes (DENV-1 to DENV-4) and aims to reduce the risk of severe dengue and hospitalisation, though it does not provide 100% immunity against every infection.
Response, status and uncertainty
The vaccine is administered in two doses, three months apart, and does not require pre-vaccination screening. Experts emphasize that vaccination should complement, not replace, mosquito bite prevention measures and be undertaken according to national guidelines.

斯里兰卡部署军用无人机应对十年来最严重的登革热 暴发 造成 56 人死亡 - 独立报

Sri Lanka deploys military drones as worst dengue outbreak in decade kills 56 people - The Independent
新闻简报斯里兰卡正在与近十年来最严重的登革热 暴发 作斗争,自 2026 年 1 月以来,报告了超过 77,000 例感染病例和 56 例死亡。由剧毒 DENV-2 毒株引起的登革热激增已使医疗设施不堪重负,促使军方部署无人机和警察来消除蚊子滋生地。医院扩大了容量并延长了工作时间来管理患者的涌入,而医生则建议症状较轻的人在家中康复。卫生官员将 暴发 归因于飓风迪特瓦留下的死水,并警告说,虽然病例可能在 8 月份有所缓解,但在 11 月份可能会再次上升。当局已清理了数千个繁殖地,并正在考虑开展 疫苗接种 活动。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该报告于2026-07-21发布。它详细介绍了自2026年1月以来持续进行的暴发,并提供了5月(8,590例)、6月(21,537例)和7月前20天(21,650例)的具体病例数。
地点与对象
The event is located in Sri Lanka, affecting a population of 22 million. High-risk transmission areas include 162 divisions across the country, with the national capital Colombo reporting the highest number of cases. Negombo, approximately 35km north of Colombo, is also identified as a worst-hit area.
事件
斯里兰卡正在经历近十年来最严重的登革热 暴发,其驱动因素是毒性更强的 DENV-2 菌株。政府任命了一支军事监测部队,并部署了空军无人机来识别积水,而警察则检查房屋和建筑工地,以消除蚊子滋生地。
规模、影响与风险
自一月份以来,已有超过 77,000 例感染报告,56 例死亡。医院增加了床位并延长了值班时间,以应对 7 月前两周涌入的 18,000 多例病例。暴发 导致卫生资源紧张,公共卫生工作者的能力已达到极限。
应对、状态与不确定性
当局已清理了约 11,000 个繁殖场所,并对 4,000 多个场所处以罚款。医生建议症状较轻的患者在家康复,以减轻医院压力。卫生官员预计,随着季风降雨减弱,8 月感染人数将减少,但警告说 11 月病例可能会再次上升。正在考虑使用登革热 疫苗。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Sri Lanka deploys military drones as worst dengue outbreak in decade kills 56 people - The Independent

News Brief
Sri Lanka is battling its worst dengue outbreak in nearly a decade, with over 77,000 infections and 56 deaths reported since January 2026. The surge, driven by a virulent DENV-2 strain, has overwhelmed healthcare facilities, prompting the military to deploy drones and police to eliminate mosquito breeding sites. Hospitals have expanded capacity and extended staff hours to manage the patient influx, while doctors advise those with mild symptoms to recover at home. Health officials attribute the outbreak to stagnant water left by Cyclone Ditwah and warn that while cases may ease in August, they could rise again in November. Authorities have cleared thousands of breeding sites and are considering a vaccination campaign.
Original source text (English)

Time
The report was published on 2026-07-21. It details an ongoing outbreak since January 2026, with specific case counts provided for May (8,590), June (21,537), and the first 20 days of July (21,650).
Location and population
The event is located in Sri Lanka, affecting a population of 22 million. High-risk transmission areas include 162 divisions across the country, with the national capital Colombo reporting the highest number of cases. Negombo, approximately 35km north of Colombo, is also identified as a worst-hit area.
Event
Sri Lanka is experiencing its worst dengue outbreak in almost a decade, driven by a more virulent DENV-2 strain. The government has appointed a military monitoring unit and deployed air force drones to identify stagnant water, while police inspect homes and construction sites to eliminate mosquito breeding grounds.
Scale, impact and risk
Since January, there have been over 77,000 reported infections and 56 deaths. Hospitals have added beds and extended duty hours to manage an influx of more than 18,000 cases in the first two weeks of July. The outbreak has strained health resources, with public health workers described as stretched to their limit.
Response, status and uncertainty
Authorities have cleared approximately 11,000 breeding sites and issued fines to over 4,000 premises. Doctors are advising patients with mild symptoms to recover at home to reduce hospital pressure. Health officials expect infections to decrease in August as monsoon rains weaken but warn cases may rise again in November. The use of a dengue vaccine is under consideration.

印度批准武田(Takeda)的 Qdenga 作为首个登革热药物 疫苗 - AllSci

India approves Takeda’s Qdenga as first dengue vaccine - AllSci
新闻简报印度已批准武田公司的 Qdenga (TAK-003) 作为其首个授权登革热 疫苗,该公司于 2026 年 7 月 21 日宣布。印度药物管制总局授予减毒活四价 疫苗 的营销授权,适用于 4 至 60 岁的个人,无论之前是否接触过登革热,从而无需进行 疫苗接种 前筛查。此次批准之际,印度面临着显着增加的登革热负担,报告病例在过去二十年中增加了十一倍以上,到 2024 年将超过 233,000 例。该授权得到了当地 III 期研究和显示持续保护的全球 TIDES 试验数据的支持。虽然武田将与当局合作推出,但有关定价、推出时间和纳入公共免疫计划的细节仍未披露。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该批准于2026年7月21日宣布。
地点与对象
印度;该授权涵盖 4 至 60 岁的个人,无论之前是否接触过登革热。
事件
印度药品监管总局授予武田 Qdenga (TAK-003) 上市许可,使其成为该国首个获得授权的登革热 疫苗。
规模、影响与风险
该批准解决了日益严重的疾病负担问题,印度每年报告的登革热病例在 20 年间增加了 11 倍以上,到 2024 年将达到 233,000 多例,尽管模型显示真正的负担要高得多。
应对、状态与不确定性
武田计划与印度监管机构和卫生当局合作制定推广计划;然而,定价、上市时间以及 疫苗 是否将进入公共免疫计划或仍仅限于私人市场分销尚未披露。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

India approves Takeda’s Qdenga as first dengue vaccine - AllSci

News Brief
India has approved Takeda’s Qdenga (TAK-003) as its first authorized dengue vaccine, the company announced on July 21, 2026. The Drug Controller General of India granted marketing authorization for the live-attenuated tetravalent vaccine for individuals aged 4 to 60, regardless of prior dengue exposure, eliminating the need for pre-vaccination screening. This approval comes as India faces a significantly growing dengue burden, with reported cases increasing more than elevenfold over the last two decades to exceed 233,000 in 2024. The authorization was supported by local Phase III studies and global TIDES trial data showing sustained protection. While Takeda will collaborate with authorities on rollout, details regarding pricing, launch timing, and integration into public immunization programs remain undisclosed.
Original source text (English)
News Pharma & Biotech Academia Resources Login Sign up Sign Up India approves Takeda’s Qdenga as first dengue vaccine July 21, 2026•Approvals•2 Minute Read Takeda said India’s Drug Controller General of India has granted marketing authorization for Qdenga (TAK-003; dengue tetravalent vaccine, live, attenuated), making TAK-003 the country’s first authorized dengue vaccine. The clearance covers individuals aged 4 to 60 regardless of prior dengue exposure, without requiring pre-vaccination testing. The approval comes as India’s reported annual dengue case count has increased more than elevenfold over the past two decades, reaching more than 233,000 cases in 2024, although modeling indicates that the true disease burden is substantially higher. Qdenga is a live-attenuated tetravalent vaccine built on a dengue serotype 2 genetic backbone, into which envelope and pre-membrane genes from the other three serotypes have been substituted, generating four chimeric viruses intended to elicit immunity against all four circulating strains. Unlike Dengvaxia, Qdenga is authorized without requiring confirmation of previous dengue infection. The India approval draws on DEN-302, a local Phase III study, alongside the global pivotal Phase III Tetravalent Immunization against Dengue Efficacy Study (TIDES), described by Takeda as the largest and longest efficacy trial conducted for a dengue vaccine. Seven-year follow-up from TIDES showed sustained protection against virologically confirmed dengue and dengue-related hospitalization, although efficacy varied by serotype and participants’ baseline serostatus. In terms of the dengue space, Sanofi’s Dengvaxia, a chimeric yellow fever-backbone tetravalent vaccine, remains regulatorily approved in the US and EU for people with confirmed previous dengue infection, although Sanofi is discontinuing global distribution because of low demand. Brazil’s Instituto Butantan has also advanced a live-attenuated tetravalent candidate approved domestically for a similar serostatus-agnostic population. TAK-003’s broader label — usable without pre-vaccination screening — is the principal point of differentiation in this landscape, simplifying deployment logistics in high-transmission settings where serological testing is impractical at scale. Since its 2022 launch, TAK-003 has been authorized in 43 countries, including Indonesia, Thailand, Malaysia and Vietnam, with more than 32 million doses distributed through public and private programs in Asia, Latin America and Europe. The World Health Organization’s Strategic Advisory Group of Experts on Immunization has recommended the vaccine without pre-vaccination screening since 2023, and it holds WHO prequalification, enabling procurement through UNICEF and PAHO. Takeda said it will now work with Indian regulators and health authorities on rollout planning, though the company has not disclosed pricing, launch timing, or whether the vaccine will enter India’s public immunization program rather than remaining limited to private-market distribution. The approval was announced on July 21, 2026.
Location and population
India; the authorization covers individuals aged 4 to 60 regardless of prior dengue exposure.
Event
India’s Drug Controller General of India granted marketing authorization for Takeda’s Qdenga (TAK-003), making it the country’s first authorized dengue vaccine.
Scale, impact and risk
The approval addresses a rising disease burden, with reported annual dengue cases in India increasing more than elevenfold over two decades to reach over 233,000 in 2024, though modeling suggests the true burden is substantially higher.
Response, status and uncertainty
Takeda plans to work with Indian regulators and health authorities on rollout planning; however, pricing, launch timing, and whether the vaccine will enter the public immunization program or remain limited to private-market distribution have not been disclosed.

印度批准首个针对 4 至 60 岁人群的登革热 疫苗 QDENGA - IndiaWest

India Approves First Dengue Vaccine QDENGA for People Aged 4 to 60 - IndiaWest
新闻简报印度已批准其首个登革热 疫苗、QDENGA (TAK-003),由武田生物制药公司开发。印度药品监管总局授权 4 至 60 岁的个人服用两剂 疫苗。这一批准解决了重大的公共卫生负担,因为印度占全球登革热病例的近三分之一,报告的感染病例在过去二十年中增加了 11 倍。临床试验表明,疫苗接种 治疗一年后,对确诊登革热的疗效为 80.2%,18 个月后对住院治疗的疗效为 90.4%,保护作用持续四年多。与某些替代方案不同,QDENGA 不需要 疫苗接种 预筛选。武田计划与当局和利益相关者合作,确保 疫苗 在全国范围内可用。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该批准于2026年7月21日宣布。
地点与对象
该批准由位于印度新德里的印度药物管制局局长批准。疫苗 授权用于 4 至 60 岁的人群。
事件
印度药品监管总局向武田生物制药印度公司授予该国首个登革热 疫苗 药物 QDENGA (TAK-003) 的市场授权。疫苗 是一种减毒活四价 疫苗,旨在预防所有四种 登革病毒 血清型。
规模、影响与风险
过去二十年来,印度报告的登革热病例增加了近 11 倍,几乎占全球登革热负担的三分之一。临床数据显示,一年后对病毒学确诊的登革热有 80.2% 的疗效,18 个月后对登革热相关住院的疗效为 90.4%。4.5 年时住院保障率维持在 84.1%。
应对、状态与不确定性
武田表示,将与印度监管机构和医疗保健提供商合作,支持 疫苗 的上市。疫苗 不需要 疫苗接种 前筛查,并且无论之前是否感染登革热,都可以施用。此次批准基于印度的一项 III 期研究以及一项涉及 28,000 多名参与者的更广泛的计划。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

India Approves First Dengue Vaccine QDENGA for People Aged 4 to 60 - IndiaWest

News Brief
India has approved its first dengue vaccine, QDENGA (TAK-003), developed by Takeda Biopharmaceuticals. The Drug Controller General of India authorized the two-dose vaccine for individuals aged 4 to 60 years. This approval addresses a significant public health burden, as India accounts for nearly one-third of global dengue cases, with reported infections rising 11-fold over the last two decades. Clinical trials demonstrated 80.2% efficacy against confirmed dengue one year post-vaccination and 90.4% efficacy against hospitalization after 18 months, with protection sustained for over four years. Unlike some alternatives, QDENGA does not require pre-vaccination screening. Takeda plans to collaborate with authorities and stakeholders to ensure the vaccine's availability across the country.

SUBSCRIBE Americas Politics People Business Indo America Immigration Crime Interview US Elections Community Opinion Sports India India and the world Entertainment Cinema Hollywood Television Photo/Video LifeStyle Wellness Fashion Beauty Food Music Arts/Books Horoscope Environment Classifieds Info4you Events Contact Us E-Newspaper Americas Politics People Business Indo America Immigration Crime Interview US Elections Community Opinion Sports India India and the world Entertainment Cinema Hollywood Television Photo/Video LifeStyle Wellness Fashion Beauty Food Music Arts/Books Horoscope Environment Classifieds Info4you Events Contact Us E-Newspaper Americas Politics People Business Indo America Immigration Crime Interview US Elections Community Opinion Sports India India and the world Entertainment Cinema Hollywood Television Photo/Video LifeStyle Wellness Fashion Beauty Food Music Arts/Books Horoscope Environment Classifieds Info4you Events Contact Us E-Newspaper HomeIndiaIndia Approves And Gets Its First Dengue Vaccine India Approves And Gets Its First Dengue Vaccine 0 Comments July 21, 2026 India Approves And Gets Its First Dengue Vaccine India-West News Desk NEW DELHI- India has approved its first dengue vaccine, with the Drug Controller General of India granting market authorization to Takeda Biopharmaceuticals India’s QDENGA (TAK-003), the company announced in a press release.History The approval comes against the backdrop of a rising dengue burden in the country. Reported cases have increased nearly 11-fold over the past two decades, and India accounts for almost one-third of the global dengue burden, although modelling studies suggest the actual number of infections is significantly higher than official estimates. Authorized for people aged 4 to 60 years, QDENGA is a live-attenuated tetravalent vaccine developed to protect against all four dengue virus serotypes. It is given in two doses, three months apart. Takeda said the vaccine can be administered irrespective of a person’s previous dengue infection and does not require pre-vaccination screening. Data submitted to the Indian regulator included findings from a Phase III clinical study conducted in India involving children, adolescents and adults between the ages of 4 and 60 years. According to the company, the vaccine was found to be safe, well tolerated and immunogenic in all three age groups. The approval was also supported by Takeda’s broader clinical development program, which comprised studies involving more than 28,000 participants from both dengue-endemic and non-endemic regions. Takeda said the vaccine recorded 80.2 per cent efficacy against virologically confirmed dengue one year after the second dose and 90.4 per cent efficacy against dengue-related hospitalization after 18 months. Follow-up data collected over the next several years showed continued protection, including 84.1 per cent efficacy against dengue-related hospitalization at 4.5 years and sustained protection against dengue disease and hospitalization through seven years across all four virus serotypes. The company said QDENGA has already been authorized in 43 countries across Asia, Latin America and Europe. More than 32 million doses have been distributed through public and private immunization programs. The vaccine has also received World Health Organization prequalification and is recommended by the WHO for use in dengue-endemic settings with high transmission. Takeda said it will now work with Indian regulatory authorities, healthcare providers and public health stakeholders to support the vaccine’s availability in the country.Vaccines & Immunizations Share With: No Comments Leave A Comment Cancel Reply Save my name, email, and website in this browser for the next time I comment. Sri Lanka Deploys Drones to Hunt Mosquito Breeding Sites Amid Severe Dengue Outbreak - News.by

News Brief
Sri Lankan authorities are deploying military drones and establishing a special monitoring unit to combat the country's largest dengue fever outbreak in over a decade. Since the beginning of 2026, nearly 77,000 cases and 56 deaths have been recorded, with approximately 18,000 hospitalizations in the first two weeks of July alone. The capital, Colombo, is the most affected area, and 162 districts have been identified as high-risk zones. Experts attribute the surge to the DENV-2 strain and environmental conditions following Cyclone Ditva. In response, the Ministry of Health has directed the Air Force to detect stagnant water, while police are inspecting buildings to eliminate breeding sites. Hospitals have expanded capacity and extended staff hours to manage the influx of patients.
Original source text (English)
Sri Lanka Deploys Drones to Hunt Mosquito Breeding Sites Amid Severe Dengue Outbreak 21.07.2026 22:12 Text by : Editorial office news.by Colombo, July 21, 2026 — Sri Lankan authorities have deployed drones to locate mosquito breeding sites as the country grapples with its largest dengue fever outbreak in over a decade, which has already claimed 56 lives. Since the beginning of 2026, nearly 77,000 cases of dengue have been recorded across the island nation. In response, the authorities have established a special mosquito monitoring unit under the armed forces. The National Dengue Control Unit has identified 162 districts as high-risk zones for transmission. Colombo, the capital, has recorded the highest number of cases. According to experts, nearly 75% of infections are linked to the more dangerous DENV-2 strain of the virus, which spreads rapidly. The Ministry of Health has tasked the Air Force with using drones to detect stagnant water on rooftops, which forms after heavy monsoon rains. Police have also been instructed to inspect homes, construction sites, and schools as part of a nationwide campaign to eliminate mosquito breeding grounds. Hospitals have increased bed capacity, set up special dengue wards, and extended working hours for doctors and nurses to cope with the influx of patients. In the first two weeks of July alone, around 18,000 people were hospitalized. Scientists have linked the outbreak to the cyclone “Ditva,” which struck in December and caused significant damage to infrastructure and medical facilities. The resulting high humidity created ideal conditions for mosquito breeding. Разделы : In the world Ukraine Moves to Prohibit Russian Language in Public Performances and Arts 21.07.2026 18:57 Text by: Editorial office news.by Kyiv, July 21, 2026 — A group of more than 60 members of Ukraine’s Verkhovna Rada has submitted a bill that would prohibit the public performance and display of literary works, art, and music videos in the Russian language. According to the bill’s registration card on the parliament’s website, the proposed restrictions would apply even to phonograms, videograms, and music clips containing performances in Russian. The current Law “On Culture” already bans works by authors who are or were citizens of Russia, but it does not currently restrict the language of performance itself. The draft law also introduces stricter language requirements for public officials and private performers. Members of parliament, heads of state institutions, and individuals engaged in cultural activities would be required to demonstrate proficiency in Ukrainian. Candidates for elected positions, including the presidency, would need to provide an official certificate confirming their level of Ukrainian language proficiency. In addition, the bill proposes limiting the use of Russian as a language of instruction in private educational institutions. The initiative was submitted by a group of more than 60 MPs, including Nikita Poturaev, Chairman of the Verkhovna Rada Committee on Humanitarian and Information Policy, and Sergey Babak, Chairman of the Committee on Education and Science. The document has been referred to the relevant parliamentary committee for review. Over the past several years, Ukrainian authorities have pursued a consistent policy of reducing the presence of the Russian language in public life. Local authorities in various regions have already introduced bans on Russian-language books, films, theater performances, and songs. The study of Russian has been significantly restricted in schools and universities, and students are often required to speak only Ukrainian even during breaks. There have also been public calls to switch from the Cyrillic to the Latin alphabet and to distance the country from Russian cultural and linguistic standards. At the same time, Russian remains widely used by Ukrainian citizens in everyday life, and Russian-language music continues to be popular, frequently leading to public controversies. Sri Lanka, specifically the island nation, with Colombo (the capital) recording the highest number of cases. The National Dengue Control Unit has identified 162 districts as high-risk zones.
Event
Sri Lankan authorities are responding to the country's largest dengue fever outbreak in over a decade. The Ministry of Health has tasked the Air Force with using drones to detect stagnant water, and a special mosquito monitoring unit has been established under the armed forces. Experts link the outbreak to Cyclone Ditva, which struck in December.
Scale, impact and risk
Since the start of 2026, nearly 77,000 cases have been recorded, with 56 deaths reported. Approximately 18,000 people were hospitalized in the first two weeks of July alone. Experts report that nearly 75% of infections are linked to the DENV-2 strain, which spreads rapidly.
Response, status and uncertainty
Police are inspecting homes, construction sites, and schools to eliminate breeding grounds. Hospitals have increased bed capacity, set up special dengue wards, and extended working hours for medical staff. The response involves nationwide military and police coordination to manage the outbreak.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Sri Lanka Deploys Air Force Drones as Dengue Outbreak Crosses 76,000 Cases - boldnewsonline.com

News Brief
Sri Lanka is intensifying its response to a severe dengue outbreak that has resulted in over 76,000 infections and dozens of deaths since the beginning of 2026. Described as the worst situation in nearly a decade, the surge is driven by the highly active DENV-2 strain and has placed significant pressure on the country's healthcare system. To combat the spread, authorities have deployed air force drones and military personnel to identify and eliminate mosquito breeding sites that are difficult to access through traditional inspections. Public health measures have been expanded, including hospital capacity increases and nationwide cleanup drives. Officials are urging citizens to remove stagnant water and have imposed penalties in areas where breeding sites are found. While monitoring severe cases, health authorities advise those with mild symptoms to recover at home to alleviate hospital burdens.
Original source text (English)
Bold News HomeLatest NewsTrending NewsEditor’s PicksIndiaJammu Kashmir NewsJammu NewsKashmir NewsLadakhEditorialOpinionMSMENationalEntertainmentBlogAbout UsContact Us Close the sidebar Sri Lanka Deploys Air Force Drones as Dengue Outbreak Crosses 76,000 Cases Authorities use drone technology and military support to identify mosquito breeding sites as hospitals struggle with a severe dengue surge. By Bold News Digital on July 21, 2026 Colombo: Sri Lanka has intensified its fight against a major dengue outbreak by deploying air force drones to identify mosquito breeding locations and support large-scale prevention efforts. The country is facing one of its worst dengue situations in nearly a decade, with more than 76,000 infections and dozens of deaths reported since the beginning of 2026. The outbreak has placed additional pressure on healthcare facilities, with hospitals witnessing a sharp rise in dengue patients. Health authorities have expanded preventive measures as the highly active DENV-2 strain continues to spread. Drone Technology Used to Locate Breeding Areas The Sri Lankan government has introduced drone surveillance to identify mosquito breeding grounds that are difficult to detect through traditional inspections. Air force teams are using aerial monitoring to locate areas where stagnant water may allow mosquito populations to grow. The initiative aims to improve early detection and support targeted cleaning operations. Authorities have also involved military personnel in inspection drives and mosquito-control campaigns across affected regions. Hospitals Face Growing Pressure The increasing number of dengue infections has created challenges for hospitals and healthcare workers. Medical facilities have expanded capacity to manage the growing number of patients, while health officials have advised people with mild symptoms to recover at home when possible. Healthcare workers are closely monitoring severe cases, especially those involving complications such as low platelet counts, dehydration, and internal bleeding risks. Climate Conditions Contribute to Dengue Spread Health experts have linked the rise in dengue cases to favourable mosquito breeding conditions following heavy rains and environmental disruptions. Authorities have increased public awareness campaigns, urging citizens to remove stagnant water from homes, workplaces, and public spaces. Officials are also conducting cleanup operations and imposing penalties in areas where mosquito breeding sites are discovered. Thousands of locations have already been cleared as part of the national response campaign. Public Cooperation Remains Key Health authorities have stressed that government action alone cannot control dengue transmission without public participation. Residents have been encouraged to maintain cleanliness, use mosquito protection measures, and immediately seek medical advice if symptoms such as high fever, body pain, or weakness appear. With continued monitoring and preventive efforts, Sri Lankan authorities hope to reduce the spread of dengue and ease pressure on the healthcare system in the coming weeks. Scale, impact and risk

审查警告称,2 型糖尿病患者患登革热的风险更高;专家权衡

People with type 2 diabetes face higher risk of dengue, warns review; experts weigh in
新闻简报Jothydev Kesavadev 博士领导的一篇新综述论文强调,2 型糖尿病患者因登革热感染而出现严重、危及生命的并发症的风险明显更高。该研究预计发表在《国际糖尿病与技术杂志》上,该研究解释说,持续高血糖会损害免疫功能并损害血管,增加对登革出血热和登革休克综合征的易感性。因此,作者建议将登革热 疫苗接种 纳入印度等流行地区人群的常规糖尿病护理中。虽然观察性研究支持糖尿病与 重症登革热 结果之间的关联,但专家强调,这并不能证明因果关系。他们敦促患者严格控制血糖水平,防止蚊虫叮咬,如果出现持续呕吐或出血等症状,请立即就医。
查看新闻五要素
时间
由 Indian Express Lifestyle Desk 于 2026 年 7 月 21 日发布。
地点与对象
该审查重点关注 2 型糖尿病患者,特别强调印度等登革热流行地区的弱势群体。
事件
由 Jothydev Kesavadev 博士领导的一篇评论论文预计将在《国际糖尿病与技术杂志》上发表,该论文警告说,2 型糖尿病患者面临更高的 重症登革热 并发症风险。该论文建议将登革热 疫苗接种 纳入常规糖尿病护理中。
规模、影响与风险
该审查表明,由于高血糖导致免疫功能受损和血管损伤,糖尿病患者更容易感染重症登革热,包括登革出血热和登革休克综合症。专家指出,并发症的风险高出 2-4 倍,但因果关系尚未得到证实。
应对、状态与不确定性
专家推荐疫苗接种,预防蚊虫叮咬,严格血糖管理。他们建议尽早就诊症状并仔细监测血糖水平和血小板计数。该评价基于现有的观察性研究和荟萃分析,而不是随机试验。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

People with type 2 diabetes face higher risk of dengue, warns review; experts weigh in

News Brief
A new review paper led by Dr. Jothydev Kesavadev highlights that individuals with Type 2 diabetes are at a significantly higher risk of developing severe, life-threatening complications from dengue infection. The study, slated for publication in the International Journal of Diabetes & Technology, explains that persistently high blood sugar impairs immune function and damages blood vessels, increasing susceptibility to dengue haemorrhagic fever and dengue shock syndrome. Consequently, the authors recommend integrating dengue vaccination into routine diabetes care for populations in endemic regions like India. While observational studies support the association between diabetes and severe dengue outcomes, experts emphasize that this does not prove causation. They urge patients to manage blood sugar levels rigorously, prevent mosquito bites, and seek immediate medical attention if symptoms such as persistent vomiting or bleeding occur.
Original source text (English)
People with type 2 diabetes face higher risk of dengue, warns review; experts weigh in A review paper highlights why high blood sugar may increase the risk of severe dengue and the warning signs people with diabetes should never ignore By: Lifestyle Desk 4 min read New Delhi Jul 21, 2026 06:00 AM IST Let's understand alla bout diabetes and dengue link (Photo: AI Generated) People with Type 2 diabetes face a higher risk of severe, life-threatening dengue complications, according to a review paper slated for publication in the International Journal of Diabetes & Technology , which calls for integrating dengue vaccination into routine diabetes care to protect vulnerable populations in dengue-endemic regions better. The study authors note that persistently high blood sugar impairs immune function and damages blood vessels, which makes people with diabetes more susceptible to severe dengue infection. According to the authors, such patients are more likely to develop an exaggerated inflammatory response, or a “cytokine storm”, which can result in dengue haemorrhagic fever, internal bleeding and, in severe cases, dengue shock syndrome, a potentially fatal condition marked by a sharp fall in blood pressure. DISCLAIMER: This article is based on information from the public domain and/or the experts we spoke to. Always consult your health practitioner before starting any routine. Diabetologist Dr Jothydev Kesavadev led the paper, which was co-authored by several leading diabetes experts from across the country, along with Dr Niti Pall, President-Elect of the International Diabetes Federation, Europe. “In India, there are more than 100 million people affected by diabetes. And among diabetics, there is a close to 2-4 times risk of developing heart attack, stroke, and multiple other infections and complications. Despite treatment, in 80-90 per cent of people, glucose remains high in our country. In our recommendation, we have mentioned that the dengue vaccine should be a part of routine care in diabetes,” Dr Jothydev told indianexpress.com . Here’s what you should note. Dr Rajiv Kovil, diabetes and obesity specialist at Zandra Healthcare, said that this is not a randomised trial but a review of existing evidence. “International observational studies and meta-analyses consistently suggest that people with diabetes who contract dengue have a higher likelihood of severe disease and possibly death. However, association does not prove causation, and the risk may also reflect age, obesity, kidney disease , hypertension or poor glycaemic control,” said Dr Kovil. Story continues below this ad Symptoms to watch out for. The initial symptoms are similar for anyone with dengue and include high fever, severe body aches, headache, pain behind the eyes, nausea, and skin rash. “However, people with diabetes should be particularly aware if they experience persistent vomiting, severe abdominal pain, unusual drowsiness, bleeding from the gums or nose, reduced urine output, dizziness, or sudden weakness. They should also check their blood glucose more often because infections can cause unpredictable spikes or, in some cases, low sugar episodes due to poor food intake,” said Dr Vijay Negalur, HoD diabetology, KIMS Hospitals, Thane. Hydration is crucial (Photo: Getty Images/Thinkstock) Prevention Prevention starts with avoiding mosquito bites by using repellents, wearing long-sleeved clothing, and getting rid of stagnant water around the home. Dr Negalur said that it is equally important to keep diabetes well-managed since stable blood sugar supports a stronger immune response . “Stay well-hydrated, eat balanced meals, and don’t ignore a fever that lasts more than a day or two. Anyone with diabetes who shows symptoms of dengue should seek medical advice early rather than wait for the illness to worsen,” said Dr Negalur. “Vaccination recommendations must follow approved indications and national policy,” added Dr Kovil. If someone with diabetes is diagnosed with dengue, are there any special treatment or monitoring considerations? Story continues below this ad Dr Negalur noted that some diabetes medications might need “temporary adjustments” based on the patient’s condition, appetite, and hydration status. “Timely medical supervision and regular monitoring of platelet counts, blood glucose, and overall health can greatly lower the risk of serious complications and support a smoother recovery,” said Dr Negalur. DISCLAIMER: This article is based on information from the public domain and/or the experts we spoke to. Location and population
The review focuses on people with Type 2 diabetes, specifically highlighting the vulnerable population in dengue-endemic regions such as India.
Event
A review paper led by Dr. Jothydev Kesavadev, slated for publication in the International Journal of Diabetes & Technology, warns that individuals with Type 2 diabetes face a higher risk of severe dengue complications. The paper recommends integrating dengue vaccination into routine diabetes care.
Scale, impact and risk
The review indicates that people with diabetes are more susceptible to severe dengue infection, including dengue haemorrhagic fever and dengue shock syndrome, due to impaired immune function and blood vessel damage from high blood sugar. Experts note a 2-4 times higher risk of complications, though causation is not proven.
Response, status and uncertainty
Experts recommend vaccination, mosquito bite prevention, and strict blood sugar management. They advise early medical consultation for symptoms and careful monitoring of glucose levels and platelet counts. The review is based on existing observational studies and meta-analyses, not a randomized trial.

News Brief
India has introduced QDENGA, its first dengue vaccine, marking a significant development in the country's efforts to combat dengue fever. The vaccine, which targets all four dengue virus serotypes, is expected to provide protection against the disease that affects millions annually. While the exact rollout details and coverage are not specified, this initiative underscores India's commitment to addressing public health challenges posed by dengue. The introduction of QDENGA comes amid ongoing efforts to control mosquito-borne diseases in the region, though further information on implementation and accessibility is awaited.
